---

### Category 5: Comparing a new instrument against an incumbent

*General idea: Showing that a new measurement instrument is "better than" an established one is not a single statistical test but a stack of separable claims — that the two instruments agree, that their scores can legitimately be placed on a common scale, that the new one adds predictive information beyond the old one, and that these findings survive honest out-of-sample estimation on an unrestricted sample. Each claim has its own estimator, its own assumptions, and a documented failure mode, and the most common defects in this literature are treating correlation as agreement, equating instruments that measure different constructs, and asserting incremental validity from a model in which the incumbent is measured with error.*

---

- **Subcategory 5.1: Correlation is not agreement — limits of agreement, intraclass correlation, and concordance**

  - **Source:** Bland & Altman (1986); Bland & Altman (1995); Bland & Altman (1999)
    - **DOK 1 — Facts:**
      - A paper proposing that agreement between two methods of clinical measurement be assessed by plotting the difference between paired measurements against their mean, and by computing "limits of agreement" as the mean difference plus and minus 1.96 standard deviations of the differences. *(Bland & Altman, 1986, *The Lancet* 327(8476):307–310, DOI 10.1016/S0140-6736(86)90837-8.)* `T2`
      - The same authors published a separate paper arguing that plotting the difference between two methods against the value from one of the two methods (rather than against their mean) is misleading. *(Bland & Altman, 1995, *The Lancet* 346(8982):1085–1087, DOI 10.1016/S0140-6736(95)91748-9.)* `T2`
      - An extended treatment covering repeated measurements, non-constant difference-versus-magnitude relationships (heteroscedasticity), and regression-based limits of agreement. *(Bland & Altman, 1999, *Statistical Methods in Medical Research* 8(2):135–160, DOI 10.1177/096228029900800204.)* `T2`
    - **DOK 2 — Summary:** The Bland–Altman approach abandons the question "do the two instruments covary?" for the question "how far apart are they, for an individual case, and is that gap acceptable?" It produces an interval on the raw score scale rather than a unitless coefficient, which forces the analyst to state a tolerance in advance. The 1995 paper is a specific methodological correction: plotting difference against one instrument's value induces a spurious correlation, so the mean of the two must be used when neither instrument is a gold standard.
    - **Assumptions / failure mode:** The 1.96-SD interval assumes differences are approximately normally distributed and that the bias and the variability of the difference are constant across the measurement range. Failure modes: (a) heteroscedastic differences make a single interval meaningless and require transformation or regression-based limits; (b) the limits are themselves estimates with sampling error, so reporting them without confidence intervals overstates precision; (c) the method describes agreement but cannot say whether the agreement is *good enough* — that threshold must be set substantively, outside the statistics.
    - **Link to source:** [Bland & Altman 1986](https://doi.org/10.1016/S0140-6736(86)90837-8) · [Bland & Altman 1995](https://doi.org/10.1016/S0140-6736(95)91748-9) · [Bland & Altman 1999](https://doi.org/10.1177/096228029900800204)

  - **Source:** Shrout & Fleiss (1979); McGraw & Wong (1996); Koo & Li (2016)
    - **DOK 1 — Facts:**
      - A paper defining a family of intraclass correlation coefficients for rater reliability distinguished by the underlying ANOVA model (one-way random, two-way random, two-way mixed), conventionally labelled ICC(1,1), ICC(2,1), ICC(3,1) and their k-rater averages. *(Shrout & Fleiss, 1979, *Psychological Bulletin* 86(2):420–428, DOI 10.1037/0033-2909.86.2.420.)* `T2`
      - A paper reformulating intraclass correlation coefficients and distinguishing coefficients defined for *absolute agreement* from coefficients defined for *consistency*. *(McGraw & Wong, 1996, *Psychological Methods* 1(1):30–46, DOI 10.1037/1082-989X.1.1.30.)* `T2`
      - A guideline article stating that there are 10 forms of ICC, that each form involves distinct assumptions and yields different interpretations, and that researchers should explicitly specify the model, type, and definition used. *(Koo & Li, 2016, *Journal of Chiropractic Medicine* 15(2):155–163, DOI 10.1016/j.jcm.2016.02.012.)* `T1`
      - The same guideline proposes that, judged on the 95% confidence interval of the ICC estimate, values below 0.5 indicate poor reliability, 0.5–0.75 moderate, 0.75–0.90 good, and above 0.90 excellent. *(Koo & Li, 2016, as above.)* `T1`
    - **DOK 2 — Summary:** "ICC" names a family, not a statistic. The choice among forms is determined by the study design (are the same two instruments applied to every case, or a random selection?) and by the question (do I care that the instruments rank cases identically, or that they produce the same number?). The consistency-versus-absolute-agreement distinction is the one that matters for head-to-head instrument comparison: a consistency ICC will look excellent even when one instrument is systematically several points higher than the other, because it absorbs the systematic offset into the model rather than penalising it.
    - **Assumptions / failure mode:** ICC forms assume the appropriate ANOVA model holds and that variance components are estimable; the absolute-agreement forms additionally assume that systematic between-instrument differences should count as disagreement. Failure modes: (a) reporting "ICC = 0.92" without specifying the form makes the number uninterpretable and non-reproducible; (b) ICC is a ratio of between-subject variance to total variance, so it inflates in heterogeneous samples and deflates in homogeneous ones — an ICC computed on a wide-ability sample will exceed the same instruments' ICC on an already-selected narrow-range sample, which is a range-restriction artefact rather than a property of the instruments (see Subcategory 5.6).
    - **Link to source:** [Shrout & Fleiss 1979](https://doi.org/10.1037/0033-2909.86.2.420) · [McGraw & Wong 1996](https://doi.org/10.1037/1082-989X.1.1.30) · [Koo & Li 2016](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jcm.2016.02.012)

  - **Source:** Lin (1989); Nickerson (1997); Barnhart, Haber & Lin (2007)
    - **DOK 1 — Facts:**
      - A paper introducing the concordance correlation coefficient, an index of reproducibility that evaluates agreement by the departure of paired observations from the 45-degree line through the origin. *(Lin, 1989, *Biometrics* 45(1):255–268, DOI 10.2307/2532051.)* `T2`
      - A correction to that paper was published in the same journal. *(Corrections, 2000, *Biometrics* 56(1):324–325, DOI 10.1111/j.0006-341X.2000.00324.x.)* `T2`
      - A published note critically examining the concordance correlation coefficient. *(Nickerson, 1997, "A Note on 'A Concordance Correlation Coefficient to Evaluate Reproducibility'," *Biometrics* 53(4):1503–1507, DOI 10.2307/2533516.)* `T2`
      - A review article surveying methods for assessing agreement with continuous measurements, co-authored by the originator of the concordance correlation coefficient. *(Barnhart, Haber & Lin, 2007, *Journal of Biopharmaceutical Statistics* 17(4):529–569, DOI 10.1080/10543400701376480.)* `T1`
    - **DOK 2 — Summary:** Lin's coefficient is best understood as Pearson's correlation multiplied by a bias-correction factor: it rewards an instrument pair for being both linearly related *and* on the same scale with the same mean. It gives a single unitless number where Bland–Altman gives an interval, which makes it convenient for summary tables and dangerous as a sole report, because a single number hides whether the disagreement is a fixed offset (correctable by rescaling) or proportional drift (not correctable by rescaling).
    - **Assumptions / failure mode:** The coefficient's standard form assumes a bivariate normal distribution for inference, and it is defined relative to the identity line, so it is not appropriate when the two instruments are legitimately on different scales. Failure modes: (a) like the ICC, it depends on between-subject variance and therefore on the sample's heterogeneity, so it is not comparable across studies with different sampling; (b) the published critique in the same journal indicates that its interpretation as a general agreement index has been contested in the peer-reviewed literature — it should be reported alongside, not instead of, a limits-of-agreement analysis.
    - **Link to source:** [Lin 1989](https://doi.org/10.2307/2532051) · [Nickerson 1997](https://doi.org/10.2307/2533516) · [Barnhart, Haber & Lin 2007](https://doi.org/10.1080/10543400701376480)

  - **Source:** Kottner et al. (2011)
    - **DOK 1 — Facts:**
      - A reporting guideline for reliability and agreement studies (GRRAS) was proposed by a multi-author group. *(Kottner, Audigé, Brorson, Donner, Gajewski, Hróbjartsson, et al., 2011, *Journal of Clinical Epidemiology* 64(1):96–106, DOI 10.1016/j.jclinepi.2010.03.002.)* `T1`
    - **DOK 2 — Summary:** There exists a consensus reporting checklist specifically for reliability and agreement studies, which means the reporting expectations for a head-to-head agreement study are not a matter of the analyst's discretion — the sampling frame, the rater/instrument selection, the statistical model, and the handling of missing data are all expected to be pre-specified and disclosed.
    - **Assumptions / failure mode:** A reporting guideline governs transparency, not validity; a fully GRRAS-compliant report of a badly designed study is still a report of a badly designed study. Its failure mode in practice is checklist compliance substituting for design scrutiny.
    - **Link to source:** [GRRAS](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jclinepi.2010.03.002)

---

- **Subcategory 5.2: Classification agreement — kappa, its documented paradoxes, and the alternatives**

  - **Source:** Cohen (1960); Cohen (1968); Landis & Koch (1977)
    - **DOK 1 — Facts:**
      - A paper introducing a coefficient of agreement for nominal scales that corrects observed agreement for agreement expected by chance. *(Cohen, 1960, *Educational and Psychological Measurement* 20(1):37–46, DOI 10.1177/001316446002000104.)* `T2`
      - A subsequent paper introducing weighted kappa, which provides for scaled disagreement or partial credit rather than treating all disagreements as equivalent. *(Cohen, 1968, *Psychological Bulletin* 70(4):213–220, DOI 10.1037/h0026256.)* `T2`
      - A paper on the measurement of observer agreement for categorical data, the source of the widely reproduced verbal benchmarks for kappa magnitude. *(Landis & Koch, 1977, *Biometrics* 33(1):159–174, DOI 10.2307/2529310.)* `T2`
    - **DOK 2 — Summary:** Kappa asks what fraction of the agreement beyond chance the two classifiers achieved. Weighted kappa extends this to ordered categories so that a one-band disagreement counts less than a three-band disagreement — which is the relevant form whenever the classification decision is an ordered cut on a continuous score rather than an unordered label.
    - **Assumptions / failure mode:** Kappa's chance-correction assumes the marginal distributions are the right model for "chance," which embeds the assumption that classifiers guess independently at their own observed base rates. Failure modes: (a) the quadratic-weighting choice is not neutral — quadratic weights make weighted kappa asymptotically equivalent to an intraclass correlation, so the "weighted kappa" reported can be silently a different statistic than the "kappa" a reader imagines; (b) the Landis–Koch verbal labels are conventions from a single 1977 paper with no distributional justification, and treating them as thresholds imports an arbitrary standard.
    - **Link to source:** [Cohen 1960](https://doi.org/10.1177/001316446002000104) · [Cohen 1968](https://doi.org/10.1037/h0026256) · [Landis & Koch 1977](https://doi.org/10.2307/2529310)

  - **Source:** Feinstein & Cicchetti (1990); Cicchetti & Feinstein (1990); Byrt, Bishop & Carlin (1993)
    - **DOK 1 — Facts:**
      - A two-part paper titled "High agreement but low kappa: I. The problems of two paradoxes," documenting that kappa can be low despite high observed agreement. *(Feinstein & Cicchetti, 1990, *Journal of Clinical Epidemiology* 43(6):543–549, DOI 10.1016/0895-4356(90)90158-L.)* `T2`
      - The companion paper, "High agreement but low kappa: II. Resolving the paradoxes." *(Cicchetti & Feinstein, 1990, *Journal of Clinical Epidemiology* 43(6):551–558, DOI 10.1016/0895-4356(90)90159-M.)* `T2`
      - A paper titled "Bias, prevalence and kappa," which formalises the two distortions as a prevalence index and a bias index and proposes a prevalence-adjusted bias-adjusted kappa. *(Byrt, Bishop & Carlin, 1993, *Journal of Clinical Epidemiology* 46(5):423–429, DOI 10.1016/0895-4356(93)90018-V.)* `T2`
    - **DOK 2 — Summary:** Kappa has two named, published pathologies. The prevalence paradox: when one category is rare — which is definitionally the case for any selective classification decision — the chance-agreement term becomes large and kappa collapses even though the two classifiers agree on nearly every case. The bias paradox: when the two classifiers have systematically different marginal rates, kappa can move in a counterintuitive direction. Because a selection decision produces exactly the skewed marginals that trigger the prevalence paradox, kappa is arguably at its *least* trustworthy precisely in the setting where a new screener is compared to an incumbent screener on a small selected fraction.
    - **Assumptions / failure mode:** The prevalence- and bias-adjusted variant assumes it is legitimate to hypothetically rebalance the marginals, which changes what the coefficient estimates rather than fixing kappa. Failure mode: reporting a single adjusted kappa without also reporting observed agreement, the full contingency table, and the two marginal rates leaves the reader unable to tell which of the paradoxes is operating.
    - **Link to source:** [Feinstein & Cicchetti I](https://doi.org/10.1016/0895-4356(90)90158-L) · [Cicchetti & Feinstein II](https://doi.org/10.1016/0895-4356(90)90159-M) · [Byrt, Bishop & Carlin 1993](https://doi.org/10.1016/0895-4356(93)90018-V)

  - **Source:** Gwet (2008); Wongpakaran, Wongpakaran, Wedding & Gwet (2013)
    - **DOK 1 — Facts:**
      - A paper stating that pi and kappa statistics "occasionally yield unexpected results in situations known as the paradoxes of kappa," exploring the origin of those limitations, and introducing an alternative agreement coefficient designated AC1, together with variance estimators whose validity does not depend on an assumption of independence between raters. *(Gwet, 2008, *British Journal of Mathematical and Statistical Psychology* 61(1):29–48, DOI 10.1348/000711006X126600.)* `T2` `[COI: the author develops and commercially distributes agreement-coefficient software and self-published reference texts on inter-rater reliability]`
      - An empirical comparison of Cohen's kappa and Gwet's AC1 conducted with personality-disorder samples. *(Wongpakaran, Wongpakaran, Wedding & Gwet, 2013, *BMC Medical Research Methodology* 13:61, DOI 10.1186/1471-2288-13-61.)* `T2` `[COI: the originator of AC1 is a co-author of this comparative evaluation of AC1]`
    - **DOK 2 — Summary:** AC1 replaces kappa's marginal-product model of chance agreement with a different chance model that is designed not to explode when one category is rare. It is the standard remedy proposed for the prevalence paradox. The important caveat is provenance: the coefficient's principal advocate is also its author and has a commercial interest in the associated software, so an independent replication of the AC1-versus-kappa comparison carries more evidential weight than the originator's own comparisons.
    - **Assumptions / failure mode:** AC1 assumes a specific alternative model of chance agreement; it is not assumption-free, only differently assumed. Failure mode: switching from kappa to AC1 *after* seeing that kappa was disappointing is an analytic degree of freedom that inflates false-positive rates (see Subcategory 5.5) — the coefficient must be pre-specified.
    - **Link to source:** [Gwet 2008](https://doi.org/10.1348/000711006X126600) · [Wongpakaran et al. 2013](https://doi.org/10.1186/1471-2288-13-61)

  - **Source:** Krippendorff (1970); Hayes & Krippendorff (2007); McNemar (1947)
    - **DOK 1 — Facts:**
      - A paper on estimating the reliability, systematic error, and random error of interval data. *(Krippendorff, 1970, *Educational and Psychological Measurement* 30(1):61–70, DOI 10.1177/001316447003000105.)* `T2`
      - A paper responding to the call for a standard reliability measure for coding data, presenting Krippendorff's alpha and computational resources for it. *(Hayes & Krippendorff, 2007, *Communication Methods and Measures* 1(1):77–89, DOI 10.1080/19312450709336664.)* `T2`
      - A note on the sampling error of the difference between correlated proportions or percentages — the source of the test now known as McNemar's test. *(McNemar, 1947, *Psychometrika* 12(2):153–157, DOI 10.1007/BF02295996.)* `T2`
    - **DOK 2 — Summary:** Krippendorff's alpha is the most general of the chance-corrected agreement coefficients: it handles nominal, ordinal, interval and ratio data, any number of raters, and missing observations, which makes it the natural choice when the comparison design is unbalanced. Separately, McNemar's test answers a question none of the agreement coefficients answer: not "do the two instruments agree?" but "do the two instruments select at systematically different rates?" — the discordant-pairs question, which is the correct test when the concern is that a new screener flags more or fewer cases than the incumbent.
    - **Assumptions / failure mode:** Alpha assumes the chosen difference metric (nominal, ordinal, interval) matches the data's actual level of measurement; choosing the wrong metric changes the value substantially. McNemar's test assumes paired observations and is a test of marginal homogeneity only — a non-significant McNemar result is fully compatible with severe case-level disagreement, because equal *rates* of selection say nothing about whether the *same* cases were selected. Reporting McNemar alone is a documented way to make two instruments look interchangeable when they are not.
    - **Link to source:** [Krippendorff 1970](https://doi.org/10.1177/001316447003000105) · [Hayes & Krippendorff 2007](https://doi.org/10.1080/19312450709336664) · [McNemar 1947](https://doi.org/10.1007/BF02295996)

---

- **Subcategory 5.3: Equating, calibration, concordance, and moderation — and why different constructs cannot be equated**

  - **Source:** Kolen & Brennan (2014)
    - **DOK 1 — Facts:**
      - A book-length treatment titled *Test Equating, Scaling, and Linking: Methods and Practices*, third edition. *(Kolen & Brennan, 2014, Springer, DOI 10.1007/978-1-4939-0317-7.)* `T2`
    - **DOK 2 — Summary:** This is the standard reference that defines the vocabulary distinguishing equating from the broader family of linking operations and that sets out the data-collection designs (single group, single group with counterbalancing, random/equivalent groups, and the non-equivalent groups with anchor test design) under which each linking method is defensible. Any head-to-head score-comparability claim is expected to name which of these designs was used.
    - **Assumptions / failure mode:** Equating in this framework is legitimate only under a set of stated requirements — most centrally that the two forms measure the same construct with similar reliability, and that the resulting conversion is symmetric and invariant across subpopulations. Failure mode: applying equating machinery when the same-construct and equal-reliability requirements do not hold produces a conversion table that is technically computable and substantively meaningless.
    - **Link to source:** [Kolen & Brennan 2014](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4939-0317-7)

  - **Source:** Dorans (2004); Dorans & Holland (2000)
    - **DOK 1 — Facts:**
      - A paper that discusses "three types of score linkage — equating, concordance, and prediction of expected scores" and states that "statistical indices, in conjunction with rational considerations, are needed to determine whether the highest level of linkage attainable between scores from two 'tests' is the conceptual and statistical interchangeability sought by equating, the distributional similarity of concordance links, or the minimum squared loss attained by prediction of expected scores." *(Dorans, 2004, *Applied Psychological Measurement* 28(4):227–246, DOI 10.1177/0146621604265031.)* `T2` `[COI: author affiliated with a commercial testing organisation whose instruments are the paper's worked examples]`
      - The same paper applies this framework to relationships among ACT and SAT I scores and discusses "the importance of score reliability in assessing linkage possibilities." *(Dorans, 2004, as above.)* `T2` `[COI: as above]`
      - A paper on population invariance and the equatability of tests, developing the basic theory and the linear case. *(Dorans & Holland, 2000, *Journal of Educational Measurement* 37(4):281–306, DOI 10.1111/j.1745-3984.2000.tb01088.x.)* `T2` `[COI: authors affiliated with a commercial testing organisation]`
      - A paper on using subpopulation invariance to assess test score equity. *(Dorans, 2004, *Journal of Educational Measurement* 41(1):43–68, DOI 10.1111/j.1745-3984.2004.tb01158.x.)* `T2` `[COI: as above]`
    - **DOK 2 — Summary:** This is the authoritative articulation of the hierarchy the question turns on. Equating is the strongest claim — the two scores become interchangeable, and it is only available for tests built to the same specifications with similar reliability. Concordance is weaker — it maps the distributions onto each other so that equal percentiles correspond, without any claim that a case is indifferent between the two instruments. Prediction of expected scores is weakest and is asymmetric: predicting instrument B from instrument A is a different function from predicting A from B, which is precisely why regression-based "conversion tables" are not equating. Crucially, the paper's own framing is that *which* level is attainable is an empirical and rational determination, not a choice — and the subpopulation-invariance work supplies the diagnostic: if the conversion between two instruments differs across demographic subgroups, the link is not an equating, whatever it is called.
    - **Assumptions / failure mode:** Concordance assumes only distributional correspondence in the linking sample, so it is population-dependent by construction and can drift when the population changes. Failure mode, and the one this category exists to name: two instruments measuring *different constructs* can always be concorded (the arithmetic never refuses) but can never be equated, and reporting a concordance table with the language of equating — "an X on the new screener is equivalent to a Y on the incumbent" — asserts interchangeability that the design does not license.
    - **Link to source:** [Dorans 2004, Equating, Concordance, and Expectation](https://doi.org/10.1177/0146621604265031) · [Dorans & Holland 2000](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1745-3984.2000.tb01088.x) · [Dorans 2004, Subpopulation Invariance](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1745-3984.2004.tb01158.x)

  - **Source:** Pommerich, Hanson, Harris & Sconing (2004); Linn (1993); National Research Council (1999)
    - **DOK 1 — Facts:**
      - A paper titled "Issues in Conducting Linkages between Distinct Tests." *(Pommerich, Hanson, Harris & Sconing, 2004, *Applied Psychological Measurement* 28(4):247–273, DOI 10.1177/0146621604265033. A variant DOI, 10.1177/0146621604264874, is in circulation and does not resolve — see gaps below.)* `T2` `[COI: authors affiliated with a commercial testing organisation]`
      - A paper titled "Linking Results of Distinct Assessments." *(Applied Measurement in Education, 1993, 6(1):83–102, DOI 10.1207/s15324818ame0601_5. Crossref lists the author surname as "Lim"; the article is conventionally cited as Linn, R. L. — see gaps below.)* `T2`
      - A National Academies consensus study report titled *Uncommon Measures: Equivalence and Linkage Among Educational Tests*, produced in the context of congressional debate over the comparability of tests used to assess school performance. *(National Research Council, 1999, National Academy Press, DOI 10.17226/6332.)* `T1`
    - **DOK 2 — Summary:** The question of whether two tests that were not built to be linked can nonetheless be linked has been examined at the level of a national-academy consensus study, not just in journal articles — which is a signal of how contested and consequential the practice is. The recurring finding across this literature is that linkages between distinct assessments are feasible to compute but fragile to interpret, degrade as the tests' content and populations diverge, and do not support individual-level interchangeability claims.
    - **Assumptions / failure mode:** Cross-test linkages assume the linking sample is representative of the population in which the link will be used. Failure mode: a linking function estimated on one cohort or one demographic composition is applied to another, and the conversion silently shifts — the same subpopulation-invariance failure described above, now at scale.
    - **Link to source:** [Pommerich et al. 2004](https://doi.org/10.1177/0146621604265033) · [Linking Results of Distinct Assessments](https://doi.org/10.1207/s15324818ame0601_5) · [Uncommon Measures](https://doi.org/10.17226/6332)

  - **Source:** Kane (2013); AERA, APA & NCME (2014)
    - **DOK 1 — Facts:**
      - A 73-page article titled "Validating the Interpretations and Uses of Test Scores." *(Kane, 2013, *Journal of Educational Measurement* 50(1):1–73, DOI 10.1111/jedm.12000.)* `T2`
      - A jointly published professional standards document, *Standards for Educational and Psychological Testing*, issued by the American Educational Research Association, the American Psychological Association, and the National Council on Measurement in Education. *(AERA, APA & NCME, 2014, Washington, DC: AERA. No DOI located — see gaps below.)* `T1`
    - **DOK 2 — Summary:** The argument-based framing reframes the entire head-to-head comparison: validity is not a property of an instrument that can be established once, but a property of a specific interpretation and use, supported by an explicit chain of inferences each of which needs its own evidence. Under this framing, a statement of the form "the new screener correlates 0.85 with the incumbent" is not a validity claim at all — it is one piece of backing for one link in a longer argument, and the argument also has to cover scoring, generalisation, extrapolation to the real-world outcome, and the decision rule itself.
    - **Assumptions / failure mode:** The argument-based approach assumes the analyst will state the intended interpretation and use *before* gathering evidence. Failure mode: it is elastic enough that a determined advocate can construct a plausible-sounding argument for almost any instrument; its discipline comes entirely from taking seriously the obligation to identify and test the *weakest* link, which is the step most often skipped.
    - **Link to source:** [Kane 2013](https://doi.org/10.1111/jedm.12000)

- **Subcategory 5.4: Incremental validity — ΔR², nested tests, relative importance, and why the inference is fragile**

  - **Source:** Hunsley & Meyer (2003); Haynes & Lench (2003)
    - **DOK 1 — Facts:**
      - An article titled "The Incremental Validity of Psychological Testing and Assessment: Conceptual, Methodological, and Statistical Issues." *(Hunsley & Meyer, 2003, *Psychological Assessment* 15(4):446–455, DOI 10.1037/1040-3590.15.4.446.)* `T2`
      - A companion article in the same issue titled "Incremental Validity of New Clinical Assessment Measures." *(Haynes & Lench, 2003, *Psychological Assessment* 15(4):456–466, DOI 10.1037/1040-3590.15.4.456.)* `T2`
    - **DOK 2 — Summary:** Incremental validity is the formal name for the question this whole category is built around: does the new instrument explain variance in the criterion that the incumbent does not already explain? The standard operationalisation is hierarchical regression — enter the incumbent first, add the new instrument, and test whether the increase in R² (ΔR²) is significantly greater than zero, equivalently a nested-model F test, equivalently the squared semipartial correlation of the new instrument with the criterion controlling for the incumbent.
    - **Assumptions / failure mode:** The hierarchical-regression operationalisation inherits every ordinary-least-squares assumption plus one that is rarely stated: that the covariate entered first is measured without error. Failure mode: entry order is a researcher choice that determines the answer, and reporting only the order that favours the new instrument is an undisclosed analytic degree of freedom.
    - **Link to source:** [Hunsley & Meyer 2003](https://doi.org/10.1037/1040-3590.15.4.446) · [Haynes & Lench 2003](https://doi.org/10.1037/1040-3590.15.4.456)

  - **Source:** Westfall & Yarkoni (2016); Brunner & Austin (2009)
    - **DOK 1 — Facts:**
      - A paper stating that claims of incremental validity "are typically supported by measurement-level models that fail to consider the effects of measurement (un)reliability," and demonstrating "that common strategies for establishing incremental construct validity using multiple regression analysis exhibit extremely high Type I error rates under parameter regimes common in many psychological domains." *(Westfall & Yarkoni, 2016, *PLOS ONE* 11(3):e0152719, DOI 10.1371/journal.pone.0152719.)* `T2`
      - The same paper reports the counterintuitive result that "error rates are highest — in some cases approaching 100% — when sample sizes are large and reliability is moderate," and concludes that "a potentially large proportion of incremental validity claims made in the literature are spurious." *(Westfall & Yarkoni, 2016, as above.)* `T2`
      - The same paper reviews structural-equation-modelling-based statistical approaches that "appropriately control the Type I error rate when attempting to establish incremental validity." *(Westfall & Yarkoni, 2016, as above.)* `T2`
      - An independent paper documenting that when independent variables are measured with error, ordinary least squares can yield biased and inconsistent estimates and that this produces an inflation of the Type I error rate, with large-scale Monte Carlo results showing "unacceptably high Type I error rates under circumstances that could easily be encountered in practice." *(Brunner & Austin, 2009, *Canadian Journal of Statistics* 37(1):33–46, DOI 10.1002/cjs.10004.)* `T2`
    - **DOK 2 — Summary:** This is the most important critique in the category and it inverts the usual intuition about statistical power. When the incumbent instrument is entered as a covariate but is measured imperfectly — as every psychological instrument is — the regression only partials out the *reliable* part of it, leaving the residual construct variance available for the new instrument to soak up. The new instrument then appears to add unique predictive power that is in fact just the incumbent's measurement error being re-explained. Because a larger sample makes this spurious signal easier to detect, more data makes the problem *worse*, not better: the false-positive rate rises toward certainty. The consequence is that a statistically significant ΔR² is, on its own, close to uninformative about whether the new instrument measures anything new. Latent-variable models that explicitly represent the incumbent's measurement error are the remedy the authors identify.
    - **Assumptions / failure mode:** The SEM remedy assumes the measurement model is correctly specified and that multiple indicators of the incumbent construct are available — often they are not, because the incumbent is delivered as a single composite score. Failure mode when that data is unavailable: there may be no valid way to test incremental validity at all with the data in hand, and the honest report is a bounded sensitivity analysis across plausible reliability values rather than a point estimate.
    - **Link to source:** [Westfall & Yarkoni 2016](https://doi.org/10.1371/journal.pone.0152719) · [Brunner & Austin 2009](https://doi.org/10.1002/cjs.10004)

  - **Source:** Budescu (1993); Azen & Budescu (2003); Johnson & LeBreton (2004); Grömping (2006)
    - **DOK 1 — Facts:**
      - A paper introducing dominance analysis as an approach to the problem of relative importance of predictors in multiple regression. *(Budescu, 1993, *Psychological Bulletin* 114(3):542–551, DOI 10.1037/0033-2909.114.3.542.)* `T2`
      - A paper developing the dominance analysis approach for comparing predictors in multiple regression. *(Azen & Budescu, 2003, *Psychological Methods* 8(2):129–148, DOI 10.1037/1082-989X.8.2.129.)* `T2`
      - A review of the history and use of relative importance indices in organisational research. *(Johnson & LeBreton, 2004, *Organizational Research Methods* 7(3):238–257, DOI 10.1177/1094428104266510.)* `T2`
      - A paper documenting an implementation of relative importance measures for linear regression. *(Grömping, 2006, *Journal of Statistical Software* 17(1), DOI 10.18637/jss.v017.i01.)* `T2`
    - **DOK 2 — Summary:** Dominance analysis exists because ΔR² from a single entry order is not a stable measure of a predictor's importance when predictors are correlated — and a new screener and an incumbent screener are, by construction, heavily correlated. Dominance analysis averages a predictor's contribution across all possible subset models, producing an importance estimate that does not depend on an arbitrary entry order. It answers "how much of the explained variance is attributable to each instrument?" rather than "does adding this one help, given that specific baseline?"
    - **Assumptions / failure mode:** Relative importance indices are descriptive decompositions of in-sample explained variance; they do not carry a natural significance test and they do not correct for measurement error. Failure mode: they are frequently read as causal importance ("the new instrument accounts for 40% of the predictive power") when they are only variance-partitioning within one fitted model on one sample, and they inherit the measurement-error problem above in full.
    - **Link to source:** [Budescu 1993](https://doi.org/10.1037/0033-2909.114.3.542) · [Azen & Budescu 2003](https://doi.org/10.1037/1082-989X.8.2.129) · [Johnson & LeBreton 2004](https://doi.org/10.1177/1094428104266510) · [relaimpo](https://doi.org/10.18637/jss.v017.i01)

  - **Source:** DeLong, DeLong & Clarke-Pearson (1988); Pencina et al. (2008); Kerr et al. (2014); Vickers & Elkin (2006)
    - **DOK 1 — Facts:**
      - A paper presenting a nonparametric approach to comparing the areas under two or more correlated receiver operating characteristic curves. *(DeLong, DeLong & Clarke-Pearson, 1988, *Biometrics* 44(3):837–845, DOI 10.2307/2531595.)* `T2`
      - A paper on evaluating the added predictive ability of a new marker, moving "from area under the ROC curve to reclassification and beyond," which introduced the net reclassification improvement and integrated discrimination improvement. *(Pencina, D'Agostino, D'Agostino & Vasan, 2008, *Statistics in Medicine* 27(2):157–172, DOI 10.1002/sim.2929; published online 2007.)* `T2`
      - A critical review titled "Net Reclassification Indices for Evaluating Risk Prediction Instruments." *(Kerr, Wang, Janes, McClelland, Psaty & Pepe, 2014, *Epidemiology* 25(1):114–121, DOI 10.1097/EDE.0000000000000018.)* `T1`
      - A paper introducing decision curve analysis as a method for evaluating prediction models. *(Vickers & Elkin, 2006, *Medical Decision Making* 26(6):565–574, DOI 10.1177/0272989X06295361.)* `T2`
    - **DOK 2 — Summary:** When the criterion is binary rather than continuous, ΔR² is replaced by a different toolkit: a paired test for the difference between two correlated AUCs, reclassification indices that count how many cases move across a decision threshold when the new instrument is added, and decision curve analysis, which weights correct and incorrect selections by an explicitly stated ratio of harms and benefits. The last of these is the only one that forces the analyst to name the cost of a false positive relative to a false negative — which for a selection decision is the substantively decisive parameter and is usually left implicit.
    - **Assumptions / failure mode:** The paired AUC test assumes the two curves are computed on the same cases. The reclassification indices have been the subject of a published critical review in a peer-reviewed epidemiology journal, indicating their statistical properties and interpretation are contested rather than settled; in particular their category-free variants have been shown to behave poorly as inferential statistics. Failure mode: reporting a positive net reclassification improvement as though it were a validated measure of clinical or practical benefit, without the decision-analytic weighting that would make "benefit" a defined quantity.
    - **Link to source:** [DeLong et al. 1988](https://doi.org/10.2307/2531595) · [Pencina et al. 2008](https://doi.org/10.1002/sim.2929) · [Kerr et al. 2014](https://doi.org/10.1097/EDE.0000000000000018) · [Vickers & Elkin 2006](https://doi.org/10.1177/0272989X06295361)

- **Subcategory 5.5: Overfitting and honest performance estimation**

  - **Source:** Efron (1983); Harrell, Lee & Mark (1996); Steyerberg et al. (2001); Copas (1983)
    - **DOK 1 — Facts:**
      - A paper on estimating the error rate of a prediction rule, presenting an improvement on cross-validation via bootstrap methods. *(Efron, 1983, *Journal of the American Statistical Association* 78(382):316–331, DOI 10.1080/01621459.1983.10477973.)* `T2`
      - A paper on multivariable prognostic models covering "issues in developing models, evaluating assumptions and adequacy, and measuring and reducing errors." *(Harrell, Lee & Mark, 1996, *Statistics in Medicine* 15(4):361–387, DOI 10.1002/(SICI)1097-0258(19960229)15:4<361::AID-SIM168>3.0.CO;2-4.)* `T2`
      - A paper on internal validation of predictive models comparing resampling-based approaches. *(Steyerberg, Harrell, Borsboom, Eijkemans, Vergouwe & Habbema, 2001, *Journal of Clinical Epidemiology* 54(8):774–781, DOI 10.1016/S0895-4356(01)00341-9.)* `T2`
      - A paper on regression, prediction and shrinkage. *(Copas, 1983, *Journal of the Royal Statistical Society Series B* 45(3):311–335, DOI 10.1111/j.2517-6161.1983.tb01258.x.)* `T2`
    - **DOK 2 — Summary:** An R² or a ΔR² computed on the same data used to fit the model is an optimistic estimate by construction, because the fitting procedure spent some of its degrees of freedom on that sample's noise. The established remedies are the bootstrap optimism correction — repeatedly refit on bootstrap resamples, measure how much better the model does on the resample it was fitted to than on the original data, and subtract that average optimism — and shrinkage, which pulls fitted coefficients toward zero by a factor estimated from the same logic. Split-sample validation is the weakest of the options because it discards data and is itself high-variance.
    - **Assumptions / failure mode:** Bootstrap optimism correction assumes the *entire* modelling procedure, including any variable selection, is repeated inside each bootstrap loop. Failure mode: selecting predictors once on the full data and then bootstrapping only the final model corrects almost none of the optimism, and is a common and easily missed error.
    - **Link to source:** [Efron 1983](https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1983.10477973) · [Harrell, Lee & Mark 1996](https://doi.org/10.1002/(SICI)1097-0258(19960229)15:4%3C361::AID-SIM168%3E3.0.CO;2-4) · [Steyerberg et al. 2001](https://doi.org/10.1016/S0895-4356(01)00341-9) · [Copas 1983](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.2517-6161.1983.tb01258.x)

  - **Source:** Varma & Simon (2006); Yarkoni & Westfall (2017)
    - **DOK 1 — Facts:**
      - A study that used cross-validation to select classifier parameters on datasets constructed with "no difference in the distribution of the features between the two classes," and found "the CV error estimate for the classifier with the optimal parameters was found to be a substantially biased estimate of the true error that the classifier would incur on independent data." *(Varma & Simon, 2006, *BMC Bioinformatics* 7:91, DOI 10.1186/1471-2105-7-91.)* `T2`
      - The same study evaluated "a nested CV procedure, where an inner CV loop is used to perform the tuning of the parameters while an outer CV is used to compute an estimate of the error." *(Varma & Simon, 2006, as above.)* `T2`
      - An article titled "Choosing Prediction Over Explanation in Psychology: Lessons From Machine Learning." *(Yarkoni & Westfall, 2017, *Perspectives on Psychological Science* 12(6):1100–1122, DOI 10.1177/1745691617693393.)* `T2`
    - **DOK 2 — Summary:** Single-loop cross-validation stops being an honest estimate the moment it is also used to choose something — a threshold, a scoring rule, a set of items, a coefficient. Once the cross-validation error has informed a decision, it has become an in-sample quantity for that decision. Nested cross-validation restores honesty by wrapping the entire tuning process inside an outer loop that never sees the tuning. The demonstration that this bias appears even on data with no real signal at all is the strongest form of the argument.
    - **Assumptions / failure mode:** Cross-validation assumes the folds are independent draws from the same population as the deployment population. Failure mode in instrument comparison: cases are frequently clustered (by site, cohort, administration session, or scorer), and random-fold cross-validation splits those clusters across folds, leaking cluster-level information and producing an optimistic estimate — grouped or blocked folds are required.
    - **Link to source:** [Varma & Simon 2006](https://doi.org/10.1186/1471-2105-7-91) · [Yarkoni & Westfall 2017](https://doi.org/10.1177/1745691617693393)

  - **Source:** Simmons, Nelson & Simonsohn (2011); Nosek et al. (2018); Collins et al. (2015)
    - **DOK 1 — Facts:**
      - An article titled "False-Positive Psychology," concerning undisclosed flexibility in data collection and analysis. *(Simmons, Nelson & Simonsohn, 2011, *Psychological Science* 22(11):1359–1366, DOI 10.1177/0956797611417632.)* `T2`
      - An article titled "The preregistration revolution." *(Nosek, Ebersole, DeHaven & Mellor, 2018, *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences* 115(11):2600–2606, DOI 10.1073/pnas.1708274114.)* `T2`
      - A reporting standard titled "Transparent Reporting of a multivariable prediction model for Individual Prognosis Or Diagnosis (TRIPOD): The TRIPOD Statement." *(Collins, Reitsma, Altman & Moons, 2015, *Annals of Internal Medicine* 162(1):55–63, DOI 10.7326/M14-0697.)* `T1`
    - **DOK 2 — Summary:** Every methodological choice enumerated in this document — which agreement coefficient, which ICC form, which entry order, which cut score, which subgroup, which exclusion rule — is a degree of freedom, and a head-to-head comparison typically involves dozens of them simultaneously. Pre-registering the analysis plan converts the comparison from an exploratory search over analyses into a confirmatory test, and there is an established reporting standard specifically for prediction-model studies that specifies what must be disclosed.
    - **Assumptions / failure mode:** Pre-registration only constrains the analyses it actually specifies; a vague pre-registration provides the appearance of constraint without the substance. Failure mode: pre-registering the primary comparison and then reporting a favourable secondary analysis with equal prominence, which restores the original flexibility while retaining the credibility signal.
    - **Link to source:** [Simmons et al. 2011](https://doi.org/10.1177/0956797611417632) · [Nosek et al. 2018](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1708274114) · [TRIPOD](https://doi.org/10.7326/M14-0697)

- **Subcategory 5.6: Range restriction, selection effects, and the missing-data framing**

  - **Source:** Thorndike (1949); Sackett & Yang (2000)
    - **DOK 1 — Facts:**
      - A book titled *Personnel Selection: Test and Measurement Techniques*, the origin of the numbered "cases" of range restriction correction that are still cited by case number. *(Thorndike, 1949, New York: Wiley. No DOI — see gaps below.)* `T2`
      - A paper developing "an expanded classification scheme for range-restriction scenarios" built from three facets: "(a) the variable(s) on which selection occurs (x, y and/or a 3rd variable z), (b) whether unrestricted variances for the relevant variables are known, and (c) whether a 3rd variable, if involved, is measured or unmeasured." *(Sackett & Yang, 2000, *Journal of Applied Psychology* 85(1):112–118, DOI 10.1037/0021-9010.85.1.112.)* `T2`
      - On the basis of those facets, the same paper "describe[s] potential solutions for 11 different range-restriction scenarios and summarize[s] research to date on these techniques." *(Sackett & Yang, 2000, as above.)* `T2`
    - **DOK 2 — Summary:** Range restriction is the structural problem in validating any selection instrument: the criterion outcome is typically only observable for the cases that were already selected, and selection was made on a variable correlated with both instruments. The correlation observed in the selected sample is therefore systematically smaller than the correlation in the full applicant population. There is not one correction but a typology of eleven scenarios, and the correction formula is only defined once the analyst can state which variable selection actually operated on and whether the unrestricted variance on that variable is known.
    - **Assumptions / failure mode:** All range-restriction corrections assume linearity and homoscedasticity in the unrestricted population, and require an externally supplied unrestricted variance that is almost never available from the restricted sample itself. Failure mode: applying a correction with a guessed or borrowed unrestricted variance transfers the guess directly into the corrected correlation, and the corrected value is then reported without the uncertainty that the guess introduced.
    - **Link to source:** [Sackett & Yang 2000](https://doi.org/10.1037/0021-9010.85.1.112)

  - **Source:** Hunter, Schmidt & Le (2006); Sackett, Lievens, Berry & Landers (2007); Wiernik & Dahlke (2020)
    - **DOK 1 — Facts:**
      - A paper titled "Implications of direct and indirect range restriction for meta-analysis methods and findings." *(Hunter, Schmidt & Le, 2006, *Journal of Applied Psychology* 91(3):594–612, DOI 10.1037/0021-9010.91.3.594.)* `T2`
      - A paper titled "A cautionary note on the effects of range restriction on predictor intercorrelations." *(Sackett, Lievens, Berry & Landers, 2007, *Journal of Applied Psychology* 92(2):538–544, DOI 10.1037/0021-9010.92.2.538.)* `T2`
      - An article titled "Obtaining Unbiased Results in Meta-Analysis: The Importance of Correcting for Statistical Artifacts." *(Wiernik & Dahlke, 2020, *Advances in Methods and Practices in Psychological Science* 3(1):94–123, DOI 10.1177/2515245919885611.)* `T1`
    - **DOK 2 — Summary:** The direct/indirect distinction is the one that bites in practice. Direct restriction means selection was made explicitly on the instrument being validated; indirect restriction means selection was made on something else correlated with it — which is the far more common real-world situation, since referral, nomination, opt-in, and prior screening all operate before the instrument is administered. The correction formulas differ, and applying the direct-restriction correction to indirectly restricted data does not fully recover the unrestricted correlation. The second paper is the one that matters most for this category specifically: range restriction distorts not only each instrument's correlation with the criterion but also *the correlation between the two instruments*, which is the denominator of every incremental-validity computation.
    - **Assumptions / failure mode:** Artefact corrections assume the artefact model is correctly specified and that the corrections are applied to the right quantities in the right order. Failure mode with direct relevance here: correcting each instrument's validity for range restriction while leaving the instrument-instrument intercorrelation uncorrected produces an internally inconsistent correlation matrix, which can yield an incremental-validity estimate that is not merely biased but arithmetically impossible.
    - **Link to source:** [Hunter, Schmidt & Le 2006](https://doi.org/10.1037/0021-9010.91.3.594) · [Sackett et al. 2007](https://doi.org/10.1037/0021-9010.92.2.538) · [Wiernik & Dahlke 2020](https://doi.org/10.1177/2515245919885611)

  - **Source:** Rubin (1976); Heckman (1979); Puhani (2000); Card & Giuliano (2016)
    - **DOK 1 — Facts:**
      - A paper titled "Inference and missing data," the origin of the missing-completely-at-random, missing-at-random, and missing-not-at-random taxonomy. *(Rubin, 1976, *Biometrika* 63(3):581–592, DOI 10.1093/biomet/63.3.581.)* `T2`
      - A paper titled "Sample Selection Bias as a Specification Error," which frames selection bias as an omitted-variable problem and proposes a two-step estimator. *(Heckman, 1979, *Econometrica* 47(1):153–161, DOI 10.2307/1912352.)* `T2`
      - A survey article titled "The Heckman Correction for Sample Selection and Its Critique." *(Puhani, 2000, *Journal of Economic Surveys* 14(1):53–68, DOI 10.1111/1467-6419.00104.)* `T1`
      - A study titled "Universal screening increases the representation of low-income and minority students in gifted education." *(Card & Giuliano, 2016, *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences* 113(48):13678–13683, DOI 10.1073/pnas.1605043113.)* `T2`
    - **DOK 2 — Summary:** Range restriction and missing data are the same problem in two vocabularies. If the criterion is unobserved for non-selected cases and selection depended only on variables in the model, the missingness is at random and likelihood-based or multiple-imputation methods are valid. If selection depended on something unobserved — including the criterion itself, or an unrecorded judgement — the missingness is not at random, and no amount of imputation recovers the truth without an explicit, untestable model of the selection mechanism. Heckman's estimator is exactly such a model. The universal-screening study is the empirical demonstration of why this is not a technicality: changing who gets assessed at all, rather than changing the instrument, materially changed who was identified — which means the sample an instrument is validated on is itself a product of an upstream selection process with its own effects.
    - **Assumptions / failure mode:** The Heckman two-step estimator assumes joint normality of the errors and, for stable identification, an exclusion restriction — a variable that affects selection but not the outcome. Failure mode, and the reason the published critique exists: without a credible exclusion restriction the model is identified only by the functional form of the normality assumption, and the resulting correction can be extremely unstable and occasionally worse than no correction at all. Missing-not-at-random adjustments are therefore a sensitivity analysis, not a fix.
    - **Link to source:** [Rubin 1976](https://doi.org/10.1093/biomet/63.3.581) · [Heckman 1979](https://doi.org/10.2307/1912352) · [Puhani 2000](https://doi.org/10.1111/1467-6419.00104) · [Card & Giuliano 2016](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1605043113)

---

- **Subcategory 5.7: Study designs and sample size for head-to-head evaluation**

  - **Source:** Kolen & Brennan (2014); Hausknecht et al. (2007); Calamia, Markon & Tranel (2012)
    - **DOK 1 — Facts:**
      - The standard equating reference sets out data-collection designs including the single-group design, the single-group design with counterbalancing, the random-groups (equivalent-groups) design, and the non-equivalent groups with anchor test design. *(Kolen & Brennan, 2014, *Test Equating, Scaling, and Linking*, 3rd ed., Springer, DOI 10.1007/978-1-4939-0317-7.)* `T2`
      - A meta-analysis titled "Retesting in selection: A meta-analysis of coaching and practice effects for tests of cognitive ability." *(Hausknecht, Halpert, Di Paolo & Moriarty Gerrard, 2007, *Journal of Applied Psychology* 92(2):373–385, DOI 10.1037/0021-9010.92.2.373.)* `T1`
      - A set of meta-analyses titled "Scoring Higher the Second Time Around: Meta-Analyses of Practice Effects in Neuropsychological Assessment." *(Calamia, Markon & Tranel, 2012, *The Clinical Neuropsychologist* 26(4):543–570, DOI 10.1080/13854046.2012.680913.)* `T1`
    - **DOK 2 — Summary:** The design choice is a trade between two threats. Administering both instruments to the same people (single group) gives maximum statistical efficiency and eliminates group differences as a confound, but contaminates the second administration with fatigue and practice. Randomly assigning people to one instrument or the other (equivalent groups) eliminates order effects but requires larger samples and relies on randomisation to equate the groups. Counterbalancing — half the sample takes A then B, half takes B then A — is the standard compromise, and it also makes the order effect itself estimable rather than merely hoped-away. That practice effects on cognitive measures are large enough to have been the subject of multiple meta-analyses is the evidence that this is not a hypothetical concern.
    - **Assumptions / failure mode:** Counterbalancing assumes the order effect is symmetric — that taking A first affects B by the same amount that taking B first affects A. Failure mode: if the two instruments differ in length, difficulty, or fatigue burden, the order effect is asymmetric, counterbalancing does not cancel it, and the residual is confounded with the instrument difference the study exists to estimate. The anchor-test design is the alternative when the two instruments cannot both be administered to the same people, but it introduces a dependence on the anchor being a valid proxy for both.
    - **Link to source:** [Kolen & Brennan 2014](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4939-0317-7) · [Hausknecht et al. 2007](https://doi.org/10.1037/0021-9010.92.2.373) · [Calamia et al. 2012](https://doi.org/10.1080/13854046.2012.680913)

  - **Source:** Bonett (2002); Zou (2012); Lu et al. (2016); Zou (2013)
    - **DOK 1 — Facts:**
      - A paper on sample size requirements for estimating intraclass correlations with desired precision. *(Bonett, 2002, *Statistics in Medicine* 21(9):1331–1335, DOI 10.1002/sim.1108.)* `T2`
      - A paper on sample size formulas for estimating intraclass correlation coefficients with precision and assurance. *(Zou, 2012, *Statistics in Medicine* 31(29):3972–3981, DOI 10.1002/sim.5466.)* `T2`
      - A paper on sample size for assessing agreement between two methods of measurement by the Bland–Altman method. *(Lu, Zhong, Liu, Miao, Li & Ji, 2016, *The International Journal of Biostatistics* 12(2), DOI 10.1515/ijb-2015-0039.)* `T2`
      - A paper on confidence interval estimation for the Bland–Altman limits of agreement with multiple observations per individual. *(Zou, 2013, *Statistical Methods in Medical Research* 22(6):630–642, DOI 10.1177/0962280211402548; published online 2011.)* `T2`
    - **DOK 2 — Summary:** Sample size for an agreement study is planned for *precision*, not for power against a null hypothesis, because the deliverable is an interval (limits of agreement, or a confidence interval on an ICC) whose width is the actual quantity of interest. This reframes the planning question from "how many cases do I need to detect a difference?" to "how many cases do I need for the limits of agreement to be narrow enough that I can act on them?" — and dedicated formulas exist for both the ICC and the Bland–Altman case.
    - **Assumptions / failure mode:** Precision-based formulas require a planning value for the very quantity being estimated (the expected ICC, or the expected standard deviation of the differences). Failure mode: an optimistic planning value yields an underpowered study whose limits of agreement are too wide to support any conclusion, and the shortfall is only discoverable after the data are collected.
    - **Link to source:** [Bonett 2002](https://doi.org/10.1002/sim.1108) · [Zou 2012](https://doi.org/10.1002/sim.5466) · [Lu et al. 2016](https://doi.org/10.1515/ijb-2015-0039) · [Zou 2013](https://doi.org/10.1177/0962280211402548)

  - **Source:** Green (1991); Maxwell (2000); Riley et al. (2019)
    - **DOK 1 — Facts:**
      - An article titled "How Many Subjects Does It Take To Do A Regression Analysis." *(Green, 1991, *Multivariate Behavioral Research* 26(3):499–510, DOI 10.1207/s15327906mbr2603_7.)* `T2`
      - An article titled "Sample size and multiple regression analysis." *(Maxwell, 2000, *Psychological Methods* 5(4):434–458, DOI 10.1037/1082-989X.5.4.434.)* `T2`
      - A paper on the minimum sample size for developing a multivariable prediction model, Part I, continuous outcomes. *(Riley, Snell, Ensor, Burke, Harrell & Moons, 2019, *Statistics in Medicine* 38(7):1262–1275, DOI 10.1002/sim.7993; published online 2018.)* `T2`
      - The companion paper, Part II, binary and time-to-event outcomes. *(Riley, Snell, Ensor, Burke, Harrell & Moons, 2019, *Statistics in Medicine* 38(7):1276–1296, DOI 10.1002/sim.7992; published online 2018.)* `T2`
    - **DOK 2 — Summary:** Detecting a small ΔR² requires substantially more cases than detecting a comparable simple correlation, because the increment is tested against a residual that the incumbent has already reduced, and because the two instruments' collinearity inflates the standard error of the new instrument's coefficient. Rules of thumb of the "ten cases per predictor" kind have been formally examined and found inadequate; the modern approach targets acceptable expected optimism and shrinkage rather than a fixed ratio.
    - **Assumptions / failure mode:** Power calculations for ΔR² require an assumed effect size for the increment, which is the least-known quantity in the study. Failure mode compounding Subcategory 5.4: a study powered adequately to detect a small ΔR² is, by the Westfall & Yarkoni result, also a study large enough to reliably detect the *spurious* increment produced by the incumbent's measurement error — so increasing sample size resolves the power problem while worsening the inferential problem, and the two must be addressed together.
    - **Link to source:** [Green 1991](https://doi.org/10.1207/s15327906mbr2603_7) · [Maxwell 2000](https://doi.org/10.1037/1082-989X.5.4.434) · [Riley et al. 2019 Part I](https://doi.org/10.1002/sim.7993) · [Riley et al. 2019 Part II](https://doi.org/10.1002/sim.7992)

- **Subcategory 5.8: Multitrait–multimethod and the convergent/discriminant framework**

  - **Source:** Campbell & Fiske (1959); Widaman (1985); Eid (2000)
    - **DOK 1 — Facts:**
      - A paper titled "Convergent and discriminant validation by the multitrait-multimethod matrix." *(Campbell & Fiske, 1959, *Psychological Bulletin* 56(2):81–105, DOI 10.1037/h0046016.)* `T2`
      - A paper on hierarchically nested covariance structure models for multitrait-multimethod data. *(Widaman, 1985, *Applied Psychological Measurement* 9(1):1–26, DOI 10.1177/014662168500900101.)* `T2`
      - A paper titled "A Multitrait-Multimethod Model with Minimal Assumptions." *(Eid, 2000, *Psychometrika* 65(2):241–261, DOI 10.1007/BF02294377.)* `T2`
    - **DOK 2 — Summary:** This is the framework that separates the two things a high correlation with the incumbent could mean. In the multitrait–multimethod design, at least two traits are each measured by at least two methods, and the pattern of correlations is diagnostic: convergent validity requires that the same trait measured by different methods correlates highly; discriminant validity requires that those same-trait/different-method correlations exceed the different-trait/same-method correlations. Applied here, it is the design that distinguishes "the new instrument measures the intended construct" from "the new instrument tracks the incumbent" — because a screener that merely imitates the incumbent will show a same-method pattern rather than a same-trait pattern. The later work supplies the confirmatory-factor-analytic machinery so that the original visual inspection of the matrix becomes a set of nested models with testable fit differences.
    - **Assumptions / failure mode:** The confirmatory MTMM models are notoriously prone to non-convergence and improper solutions (negative variance estimates), which is why the later literature developed reduced models with weaker assumptions. Failure mode: a full multitrait–multimethod design requires at least two distinct traits, and an evaluation that measures only one construct cannot produce the matrix at all — in which case discriminant validity is simply not being tested, however many correlations are reported.
    - **Link to source:** [Campbell & Fiske 1959](https://doi.org/10.1037/h0046016) · [Widaman 1985](https://doi.org/10.1177/014662168500900101) · [Eid 2000](https://doi.org/10.1007/BF02294377)

  - **Source:** Henseler, Ringle & Sarstedt (2015); Rönkkö & Cho (2022); Mokkink et al. (2010)
    - **DOK 1 — Facts:**
      - A paper titled "A new criterion for assessing discriminant validity in variance-based structural equation modeling," proposing the heterotrait–monotrait ratio of correlations. *(Henseler, Ringle & Sarstedt, 2015, *Journal of the Academy of Marketing Science* 43(1):115–135, DOI 10.1007/s11747-014-0403-8; published online 2014.)* `T2` `[COI: a co-author is a co-founder of and has a financial interest in a commercial structural-equation-modelling software product widely used to compute this criterion]`
      - A paper titled "An Updated Guideline for Assessing Discriminant Validity." *(Rönkkö & Cho, 2022, *Organizational Research Methods* 25(1):6–14, DOI 10.1177/1094428120968614; published online 2020.)* `T2`
      - A consensus paper reporting that "the COSMIN study reached international consensus on taxonomy, terminology, and definitions of measurement properties." *(Mokkink, Terwee, Patrick, Alonso, Stratford, Knol, et al., 2010, *Journal of Clinical Epidemiology* 63(7):737–745, DOI 10.1016/j.jclinepi.2010.02.006.)* `T1`
    - **DOK 2 — Summary:** Discriminant validity assessment has an active and contested modern literature: a widely adopted ratio-based criterion, a subsequent guideline article revisiting how discriminant validity should be assessed, and — separately — an international consensus taxonomy that fixes what the measurement-property terms even mean. The existence of a consensus taxonomy matters because "reliability," "agreement," "validity," and "responsiveness" are used inconsistently across fields, and a head-to-head comparison that borrows a method from one field inherits that field's definitions.
    - **Assumptions / failure mode:** Ratio-based discriminant-validity criteria depend on a threshold (commonly 0.85 or 0.90) that is a convention rather than a derived value, and they assume the constructs are reflectively measured. Failure mode: threshold-based criteria invite the same post-hoc coefficient-shopping described in Subcategory 5.2, and the published updated guideline indicates the field has not settled on a single defensible rule — so a claim of discriminant validity should state which criterion and which threshold were pre-specified.
    - **Link to source:** [Henseler et al. 2015](https://doi.org/10.1007/s11747-014-0403-8) · [Rönkkö & Cho 2022](https://doi.org/10.1177/1094428120968614) · [COSMIN](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jclinepi.2010.02.006)

Roughly half of these have a peer-reviewed software paper, which is a meaningfully stronger provenance signal than a CRAN listing alone.
    - **Assumptions / failure mode:** Package documentation is `T3` evidence about the software, not `T1`/`T2` evidence about the method — a function's existence says nothing about whether its defaults match the analyst's design. Concrete failure mode: `psych::ICC` returns all six Shrout–Fleiss forms at once and the analyst picks one afterwards, while other packages require the form to be specified up front; silently reporting whichever row was largest is the coefficient-shopping failure again, now enabled by convenient output.

Observed-score equating and IRT-based linking are the conspicuous gap: there is no Python equivalent with the maturity or the peer-reviewed provenance of the R equating packages, which is a practical argument for doing the linking analysis in R even in a predominantly Python workflow.

Failure mode specific to cross-language work: an ICC or a kappa computed in two languages can differ because the packages default to different forms or different weighting schemes, and the discrepancy is easy to misread as a data problem rather than a defaults problem.
